On 1 March, another large protest was held in [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1).[^cite_note-416] Tens of thousands of protesters gathered, marking four months since the canopy collapse with 15 minutes of silence.[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] In reference to the [Edict of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Milan) issued by the Niš-born Roman Emperor [Constantine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_I), the students drafted and presented the "Student Edict", which contains provisions on freedom, state, justice, youth, dignity, knowledge, solidarity and the future.[^cite_note-419] After the protest, columns of taxi drivers from various cities transported the students back home, free of charge.[^cite_note-420] Citizens also protested in [Inđija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In%C4%91ija), where a man threatened the protesters with a knife.[^cite_note-421] On the same day, in its evening news program, RTS aired the news coverage of the Niš protest before airing the official statement by president Vučić. Pro-government tabloids characterized such reporting as a "[coup d'etat](25914154.md)" and accused RTS of being "controlled by students".[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423]

On 2 March, students and high school pupils from [Gornji Milanovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gornji_Milanovac) blocked the [Ibar Highway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibar_Highway). The blockade lasted four hours, from 11 am to 4 pm.[^cite_note-424] In Belgrade, citizens blocked the Blue Bridge, which connects the [Konjarnik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konjarnik) and [Medaković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medakovi%C4%87,_Belgrade) neighborhoods, to express support for students' demands.[^cite_note-425] In [Krupanj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krupanj), residents protested the arrival of Miloš Vučević, who was attending an SNS party debate in that town.[^cite_note-426] The informal association of education workers, "United Education of Serbia", announced that the professors and schoolteachers will embark on an "educational relay walk", an action called "A Step to Freedom" during which, from 3 to 6 March, they will walk around 160 kilometers from Belgrade to [Čačak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%8Ca%C4%8Dak), where a large protest of education workers will take place.[^cite_note-427]

On the same day, speaking at the SNS gathering in [Bor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bor,_Serbia), president Vučić said: "Can you imagine, yesterday RTS was reporting from Niš, and it was done by some imbecile reporter from their Niš office"; Vučić later apologized for calling the correspondent an "imbecile" and RTS condemned the statement.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] 46 journalists and media workers from Niš announced that they had rejected Vučić's apology, and said that they will not follow nor report about his activities until an appropriate apology is made.[^cite_note-430]

On 3 March, the "Sloga" police union expressed its support for the "Student Edict" adopted on 1 March in Niš, describing this as a manifesto of the struggle for institutions which should serve the citizens, and not serve the interests of individuals.[^cite_note-431] The Novi Sad Police Association "Dr Rudolf Arčibald Rajs" wrote an open letter to the Minister of Interior [Ivica Dačić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivica_Da%C4%8Di%C4%87), expressing their concern about the "worrying practice in which the police, instead of applying the law and protecting public order and peace, become victims of political calculations".[^cite_note-432] Several employees of public utility companies in Novi Sad spoke to media about allegedly losing their jobs after they openly supported the protests.[^cite_note-433]

### Reprisals against education workers

On 4 March, Prime Minister Miloš Vučević said that professors and teachers who protested will not receive the first part of their February salary.[^cite_note-434] That same day, a [brawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Serbian_National_Assembly_fight?action=edit\&redlink=1) erupted during a session in the National Assembly, during which opposition MPs threw [smoke bombs](720367.md).[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-436]

The salary of education workers was reduced between 50 and 100 percent; in some cases, high school teachers were paid only 2,191 [RSD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_dinar) (less than 20 [EUR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EUR)).[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-438] The Independent Trade Union of Teachers of Serbia (NSPRS) issued an official statement: "If the wages of school employees are reduced, and that reduction is not a consequence of legally implemented and legally prescribed disciplinary proceedings against each individual employee whose wages are reduced, the persons responsible for such proceedings will be the subject of lawsuits and criminal charges filed by NSPRS and every affected employee."[^cite_note-439] An informal group of IT experts invited citizens to join the "Solidarity for Education" network and donate to help educators.[^cite_note-440]

Due to attacks by pro-government tabloids on the President of the executive board of [Assicurazioni Generali Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assicurazioni_Generali), Dragan Filipović, and the expulsion of civil society activists in January, a large event planned in Belgrade by the association was canceled, according to the magazine *Nova Ekonomija*.[^cite_note-441]

### Strikes of public-sector librarians and health workers

Employees of the [National Library of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Library_of_Serbia) announced a general strike, starting 6 March.[^cite_note-442] The employees of the "Studenica" General Hospital and the Kraljevo Health Center announced a [warning strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warning_strike) for 7 March, demanding the fulfillment of four demands. Among them is the publication of all documents about the 2015 crash of an [Mi-17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi-17) helicopter that caused seven deaths, including a sick infant transported for treatment.[^cite_note-443]

On 6 March, an incident happened in front of the Belgrade City Hall between protesters and a private security firm hired by the city. The private company in question was allegedly tied to "phantom voters" from the [Republika Srpska](347422.md) who came to vote for the SNS at the December 2023 elections. In September 2024, this company, in a consortium with two other companies, concluded a framework agreement with the City of Belgrade, worth 180 million [RSD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_dinar) for security.[^cite_note-444] On the same day, citizens from ten Belgrade municipalities, after protesting in their respective municipalities in the previous weeks, decided to hold a joint gathering in front of the prosecutor's office at the Palace of Justice, where they came in several columns. They made noise by blowing whistles and vuvuzelas and beating drums, broken up with a 15-minute pause for a moment of silence to the victims of the Novi Sad Railway Station collapse. [^cite_note-445]

On 7 March, workers of the [RB Kolubara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RB_Kolubara) mines protested in front of their office building in [Lazarevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazarevac).[^cite_note-446] In [Kosjerić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosjeri%C4%87), students blocked the local bridge.[^cite_note-447] Thousands of citizens protested in [Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kragujevac), [Kruševac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kru%C5%A1evac), [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1) and [Novi Sad](113288.md), supporting the underpaid education workers and calling for a general strike.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451] Many shops, bars and restaurants remained closed in support of the protests. In Belgrade, a huge column of people walked on the streets around the downtown area passing by government buildings. Marching students said their protest presented a 'prelude' to a massive rally planned for March 15 in Belgrade.[^cite_note-452]

### Prelude to *15. za 15* and "Students 2.0"

On the same day, a group of 30 young people gathered and set up tents in front of the president's office in [Pioneers Park, Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pioneers_Park,_Belgrade), claiming to be "Students 2.0" who want to end the protests and continue studying; however, numerous sources identified the persons as either SNS members or affiliates.[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-454][^cite_note-455]

On 8 March, farmers in [Bogatić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogati%C4%87) blocked a street with tractors and clashed with the [Žandarmerija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDandarmerija) gendarmerie unit which tried to remove them.[^cite_note-456] Soon after, they were allowed to stay and even given permission to enter the local Agricultural Fair with their tractors.[^cite_note-457] During his official visit to [Kovačica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kova%C4%8Dica), president Vučić was [booed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Booed) by dozens of local citizens, to which he responded: "They love me, man, the people, you can see how much they love me, it's a marvel! In here, 500 people are waiting for me at 20 meter distance, and 50 of them like to whistle and blow their horns, well, what am I supposed to do?".[^cite_note-458]

In [Smederevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smederevo), a large citizen's protest was organized, as well as a meeting of the SNS, simultaneously in the local Sports Center. Before the SNS gathering started, the police created a cordon around the Sports Center in order to prevent the gathered citizens from reaching the SNS meeting. The crowd then tore Vučić's poster from a nearby billboard.[^cite_note-459] In Belgrade, thousands of students congregated to [Republic Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Square_\(Belgrade\)), followed by farmers on tractors.[^cite_note-460]

On 9 March, commenting on the large protest scheduled for 15 March, president Vučić said that he expects the opposition to "organize great violence" at the rally in Belgrade, and that "everyone who participates in it will be arrested", and stated that it will "take 15 minutes, an hour or two, because the state will be able to behave like a state; there is nothing to discuss".[^cite_note-461] "The introduction of a [state of emergency](298608.md) is one of the options, depending on what will happen on the streets on 15 March Vučić wants incidents to happen", MP of the Ecological Uprising, Aleksandar Jovanović Ćuta, told *Vreme* magazine.[^cite_note-462]

On 10 March, the Independent Union of Educators of Serbia (NSPRS) filed criminal charges with the special anti-corruption departments of the higher prosecutor's offices in Belgrade, Novi Sad, Niš and Kraljevo against the outgoing Minister of Education and 12 advisors for material and financial affairs of school administrations due to the reduction of education salaries.[^cite_note-463] Hundreds of students from [Čačak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%8Ca%C4%8Dak), [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1) and [Subotica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subotica) began their march on foot towards Belgrade, with the goal of reaching it prior to the great protest scheduled for 15 March.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465]

On 10 March and into 11 March, the offices of the state-owned media broadcasters RTS and [Radio-televizija Vojvodine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Television_of_Vojvodina) were blockaded by large groups of students and citizens. Protesters also gathered outside [RTV Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTV_Kragujevac) in a show of support. Clashes between demonstrators and Žandarmerija occurred during the protest in Belgrade outside the RTS building, during which time one plainclothes policeman was injured in the eye. President Vučić met with the man that night, posting on his Instagram account that he had been injured by "Bolshevik plenum members,"[^cite_note-466] though footage of the incident shows the policeman being struck by a uniformed officer.[^cite_note-467] While Vučić and Minister Ivica Dačić claimed that the students attacked the police, officers can be seen beginning the violence by striking students with batons in video footage taken at the scene.[^cite_note-468] Protesters announced that they intended to blockade the building for 22 hours, leading RTS to state that they would broadcast from their offices in Košutnjak the following day.[^cite_note-469]

### Media censorship & attacks

Due to independent journalism and support for anti-corruption protests, independent media outlets received serious threats from supporters of the [Serbian Progressive Party](19454384.md) and [government officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_officials).[^cite_note-470]

In May 2025, Students 2.0 reportedly sent warning letters to several independent media outlets, threatening [SLAPP lawsuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_lawsuit_against_public_participation) and even [criminal charges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_charge).[^cite_note-\:2-471][^cite_note-\:0-472] The letters claimed that the media published “baseless, tendentious and offensive comments” about [President Aleksandar Vučić](13349267.md), the [Serbian Progressive Party (SNS)](19454384.md), and the Students 2.0 organization, that they believed were defamation.[^cite_note-\:0-472] The warnings preceding a criminal complaint included the names of three influencers, Aleksandar "Cile ST" Leštarić, Danica Manojlović, and Luka Lekić, who were presented as "[META](62420226.md) [Advisors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advisors)" allegedly planning to illegally suspend the accounts of [independent media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_media) on social networks.[^cite_note-473][^cite_note-\:1-474] All three influencers have distanced themselves from these claims, stating that the allegations "have no basis in truth" and that their names were deliberately misused as retaliation for their support and participation in Serbian anti-corruption protests.[^cite_note-\:1-474][^cite_note-\:2-471] Threats were described by lawyers as vague: the letters failed to specify which particular media reports were considered defamatory, and instead listed a broad set of potential legal actions without clear foundation. [^cite_note-\:2-471]

On 13 August 2025, the Independent Journalists’ Association of Serbia (NUNS) and the Association of Independent Electronic Media (ANEM) condemned [threats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threat) and attacks against journalists, calling for urgent investigation and [prosecution](440936.md).[^cite_note-\:3-475] The threats targeted the [*Danas* newspaper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danas) editorial team via [Facebook Messenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook_Messenger), warning of a “bomb or grenade” and stating that “they will come to your home a little,” while journalist Marko Dragoslavić was physically assaulted on 12 August while photographing a construction site under a work ban.[^cite_note-\:3-475] Authorities intervened, offered medical assistance, and arrested a suspect. NUNS and ANEM emphasized that such incidents constitute serious threats to media freedom and journalists’ safety, warning that impunity encourages further attacks and undermines the public's right to information.[^cite_note-\:3-475]

Students 2.0 has been criticized for repeated attacks on [journalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalist) within and around the camp, often occurring in the presence of police who reportedly failed to intervene Reported victims include reporters from KTV, Fonet, and *Insajder*, as well as a YouTuber, who were threatened, physically assaulted, or prevented from reporting.[^cite_note-\:4-476] Legal experts and former police officials have condemned the authorities’ inaction, noting that it contributed to a climate of impunity and further intimidation of the press.[^cite_note-\:4-476]

On 20 August 2025, [United Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Group) sent an urgent letter to the [OSCE Representative on Freedom of the Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OSCE_Representative_on_Freedom_of_the_Media), *Jan Braathu*, calling on the organization to demand explanations from Serbian authorities over what it described as a “hostile environment” for journalists at [N1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N1_\(TV_channel\)) and Nova.[^cite_note-\:5-477][^cite_note-\:6-478][^cite_note-\:7-479] United Media expressed deep concern that in recent months, President Aleksandar Vučić, senior government officials, and members of the ruling party have repeatedly labelled these news outlets as “terrorists,” “occupiers,” and “media poisoners,” framing them as instigators of unrest.[^cite_note-\:5-477][^cite_note-\:6-478][^cite_note-\:7-479]

According to [United Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Group), this rhetoric has fueled more than 30 incidents over the past ten months, ranging from verbal and physical attacks to death threats and interference with reporting, and many such cases remain unresolved.[^cite_note-\:5-477][^cite_note-\:6-478][^cite_note-\:7-479] They warned that such language “normalizes hatred, legitimizes violence, and creates a climate in which aggression against journalists becomes acceptable,” and urged the OSCE to publicly condemn the rhetoric, press state institutions to investigate and prosecute those responsible, and remind [Serbia](29265.md) of its international obligations to protect [media freedom](105323.md).[^cite_note-\:5-477][^cite_note-\:6-478][^cite_note-\:7-479]

### Reinforcements sent to "Students 2.0"



Members of the [JSO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Operations_Unit_\(Serbia\)) in the Pionirski park

In the improvised tent camp in Pioneers Park, the pro-government students were replaced by thugs wearing caps and hoods who attacked passersby on at least one occasion; some of these persons were filmed and [doxxed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doxxed).[^cite_note-480] The following day, a group of military veterans from the defunct [Special Operations Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Operations_Unit_\(Serbia\)) set up tents while wearing green uniforms with red berets. The unit, which was disbanded in 2003, had carried out assassinations of [Slobodan Milošević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slobodan_Milo%C5%A1evi%C4%87)'s political opponents and has been accused of involvement in war crimes during the [Kosovo war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_war). Among the veterans present was [Goran Radosavljević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goran_Radosavljevi%C4%87), the last commander of the [Special Police Units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Police_Units_\(Serbia\)), and Živojin Ivanović, a JSO commander known also as Žika Crnogorac.[^cite_note-481] A JSO veteran was seen attacking an onlooker with a stick in video footage taken at the park.[^cite_note-482]

On 12 March, professors met at the Faculty of Medicine of the [University of Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Belgrade). After a long and heated debate, they voted in favor of a general strike, which began on 17 March and would last until the students' demands are met.[^cite_note-483] On the same day, near the tent camp in Pioneers park, bypassers were physically attacked by the director of the [PUC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_utility_company) "Komunalac" from [Kula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kula,_Serbia), Marko Pešić, who is also a member of the Kula committee of the SNS.[^cite_note-484]

In solidarity with the students' demands and their protests across Serbia, students and high school students from [Valjevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valjevo) set off on a protest walk to Belgrade in the morning of 12 March to join the "15 for 15" protest on 15 March; they reached [Lazarevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lazarevac) in the evening, where they were welcomed by locals and provided accommodation.[^cite_note-485] Students who set off from [Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kragujevac) on foot for Belgrade arrived in [Rača](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ra%C4%8Da,_Serbia) on the evening of 12 March. They were met at the entrance by tractor drivers, and given a festive welcome with a folklore performance.[^cite_note-486]

On 13 March, President Vučić asked the pro-government Students 2.0 encampment in Pioneers Park to temporarily withdraw ahead of the protest in front of the National Assembly on 15 March.[^cite_note-487] That same day, a group of about a hundred people, most of whom belonged to the [Movement of Socialists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_Socialists), blocked the entrance to the office of the news channel [N1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N1_\(TV_channel\)). In a parliamentary session, [Aleksandar Vulin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandar_Vulin), an ally of Vučić, confirmed that he had organized the blockade, which lasted for several hours. He added that "it won't be difficult for them to come again."[^cite_note-488] Students travelling to Belgrade on foot from across the country continued their marches.

On 14 March, in the early morning around 150 tractors, most of them without a licence plate, surrounded the Pionirski Park where the "Students 2.0" are located.[^cite_note-489] At the same time, the National Assembly was closed until 17 March due to "safety reasons".[^cite_note-490] In the morning of the same day, all trains were cancelled due to a "bomb threat".[^cite_note-491] The [European Public Prosecutor's Office](1707190.md) (EPPO), led by [Laura Codruța Kövesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laura_Codru%C8%9Ba_K%C3%B6vesi), announced that it is investigating potential misuse of EU [IPA funds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrument_for_Pre-Accession_Assistance) regarding the Novi Sad train station.[^cite_note-492] In the evening, most of the students and citizens finished their marches and arrived in Belgrade. According to the MUP, the arrival of the students was attended by 31,000 people.[^cite_note-493] [UN Human Rights Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Human_Rights_Office) spokesperson Jeremy Laurence urged the authorities to allow the protest to take place without unwarranted interference.[^cite_note-494]

### *15. za 15*

Further information: [2025 Belgrade stampede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Belgrade_stampede)

On 15 March, the "15th for 15" protest ([Serbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_language): 15. за 15, *15. za 15*), was held in Belgrade in front of the National Assembly and at Slavija. The protest was attended by thousands of university students, citizens from across the country ,and veterans of the 63rd Parachute Brigade. Student organizers ended the protest after bottles and stones were thrown at protesters from the Students 2.0 encampment near the Serbian parliament, leading to brief yet non-violent tension between protesters and the Gendarmery.[^cite_note-495] Around the same time, at 7\:11 pm, an [LRAD was allegedly deployed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Belgrade_stampede) on peaceful protesters during a moment of silence for the victims of the Novi Sad collapse.[^cite_note-496] The MUP later denied the allegations[^cite_note-497] in spite of video footage of the incident,[^cite_note-498] as did president Vučić, who called for the prosecution of those "lying" and "spreading disinformation".[^cite_note-499] The Public Prosecutor's Office in Belgrade did not launch an investigation into use of a [sonic weapon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonic_weapon), instead issuing a statement claiming no indications of its use, citing reports from the Ministries of Defence and Health.[^cite_note-500] The number of people at the protest is disputed: the official government figure provided by MUP was 107,000, an analysis by the Archive of Public Meetings found there were between 275,000 and 325,000 present "with the possibility that the number was even higher,"[^cite_note-501] and [Božo Prelević](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bo%C5%BEo_Prelevi%C4%87?action=edit\&redlink=1), the former MUP minister, estimated there were at least half a million protesters.[^cite_note-502][^cite_note-503]

In its live coverage of the protest, the pro-government television station *Informer TV* organized a celebration. The editor-in-chief of Informer, Dragan J. Vučićević and SNS official, [Vladimir Đukanović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_%C4%90ukanovi%C4%87) were present in the studio. "There is a special song, can we play it now, on the occasion of this sound cannon. It goes like this: ["Wind, blow through the liver"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baja_Mali_Knind%C5%BEa), Đukanović asked. "This one is for the sound cannon", said Vučićević while fulfilling his guest's wish.[^cite_note-504][^cite_note-505]

On 16 March, numerous reports emerged on social media about unusual health symptoms among protesters in Serbia, including [dizziness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dizziness), [arrhythmia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrhythmia), [hearing loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearing_loss) in one or both ears,[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-Evropa-2025-507][^cite_note-508] and disturbances in pacemakers. Many citizens also suffered orthopedic injuries caused by the sonic weapon.[^cite_note-Evropa-2025-507][^cite_note-509] Serbian volleyball player Vanja Grbić initially claimed that his colleague Marko Samardžić had suffered seven heart attacks due to the weapon's effect on his pacemaker [^cite_note-510] but later revised his statement, saying Samardžić had simply fallen ill during the protests.[^cite_note-511] All of these concerns led students to protest on March 17 in front of the Emergency Center, demanding the publication of the exact number of people treated after the March 15 incident.[^cite_note-512][^cite_note-513]

On 17 March, the Judicial Authority Union announced that all employees of judicial authorities, courts, public prosecutor's offices and penal institutions will go on a one-day strike on 18 March.[^cite_note-514][^cite_note-515] On the same day, citizens of [Obrenovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obrenovac) gathered in front of the municipal building on the main square to express their dissatisfaction after a video of municipal members leaving Belgrade's Pioneer Park was released on social media. The protesters threw eggs at the entrance door of the municipality and its employees. The assistant to the mayor in charge of social activities and education, Dragan Blažić Vivak, was hit in the head with several eggs.[^cite_note-516] Six protesters were arrested after the incident.[^cite_note-517]

On 18 March, in a TV interview, president Vučić said "Faculty professors who do nothing, except participate in blockades, will get potatoes. Tomorrow, I think, this part will be paid out for them. They will get nothing. There is currently joy in millions of homes in Serbia. People do not want anyone to rob them."[^cite_note-518][^cite_note-519]

On 19 March, several dozen citizens of [Sremska Mitrovica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sremska_Mitrovica) gathered in front of the local City Administration building, dissatisfied with the fact that a large number of city officials, including Mayor Branislav Nedimović, falsely presented themselves as students at a camp in Pioneer Park in Belgrade. Many protesters [spat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spitting) on Nedimović.[^cite_note-520][^cite_note-521] Members of the [European Parliament](9581.md) Vladimir Prebilič and Gordan Bosanac from the Greens group addressed a letter to [European Commission](9974.md) President [Ursula von der Leyen](2991932.md) expressing deep concern about her scheduled meeting with President Vučić next week.[^cite_note-522]

On 21 March, a planned SNS meeting in Niš was cancelled after protesters threw eggs at party officials, leading police to blockade the historic [building of the Officers Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Building_of_the_Officers_Club?action=edit\&redlink=1) where the meeting was to be held.[^cite_note-523] Ana Brnabić, Miloš Vučević, and [Darko Glišić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darko_Gli%C5%A1i%C4%87_\(politician\)) were supposed to be in attendance; mayor [Dragoslav Pavlović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragoslav_Pavlovi%C4%87?action=edit\&redlink=1), who was also planning on attending, was splashed with water and pelted with eggs.[^cite_note-524] That same day, around 10.000 people attended a commemorative walk in Novi Sad to pay their respects to Vukašin Crnčević, a 19-year-old who died earlier that day from serious injuries sustained four months earlier in the collapse of the railway station canopy in Novi Sad.[^cite_note-525] In [Bogatić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogati%C4%87), protesters threw eggs at the municipality building and at the restaurant owned by the local SNS municipal functionary.[^cite_note-526] That same day, the mother of one of the victim of the canopy collapse informed the public that she had received threats and photoshopped pictures of her dead son because of her support for the protesters; she reported the case to the police,[^cite_note-527][^cite_note-528] who dismissed these claims.[^cite_note-529]

### First zbor

On 22 March, the first Zbor was announced in Kragujevac.[^cite_note-530] Citizens also organized "Zbor" gatherings, based on the principals of direct vote, in [Bogatić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogati%C4%87), [Trstenik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trstenik,_Serbia), [Arilje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arilje), [Pančevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan%C4%8Devo), [Čačak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%8Ca%C4%8Dak), [Vlasotince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlasotince), [Vršac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vr%C5%A1ac), [Zrenjanin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zrenjanin), [Šid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%A0id), [Novi Sad](113288.md), [Smederevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smederevo), [Bač](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba%C4%8D,_Serbia), [Pirot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirot), [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1), [Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kragujevac), and several [Belgrade](55904.md) municipalities.[^cite_note-531] On the same day, the employees of Pantransport, a public transport company from Pančevo, announced that they will hold a one-day strike on 28 March and stop all transportation on city and intercity lines. The decision was made in support of the students, but also because of the dissatisfaction of drivers who no longer want to transport SNS activists to rallies.[^cite_note-532]

In Čačak, a group of student protesters from the local Technical Faculty were attacked by a group of young, masked men.[^cite_note-533][^cite_note-534] Hundreds of people protested in response, and some of the protesters stormed the city administration building, but left soon afterwards.[^cite_note-535][^cite_note-536] That same day, president Vučić told the gathered citizens at the National Theater in [Leskovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leskovac): "Those police officers who do not want to protect order and the law will be changed, those prosecutors who do not want to protect order and the law will be changed".[^cite_note-537] According to a statement signed by 220 judges and prosecutors in Serbia, "by declaring that those prosecutors who do not protect order and the law will be replaced, the President of the Republic has written a new page in the twelve-year enslavement and humiliation of the judiciary system".[^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539]

On 23 March, in the Belgrade municipalities of [Vračar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vra%C4%8Dar) and [Zvezdara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zvezdara), citizens gathered around SNS booths and held a silent vigil for 17 minutes, honoring the victims of the [Kočani nightclub fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ko%C4%8Dani_nightclub_fire) in [North Macedonia](23564616.md), after which they started chanting anti-SNS slogans. The police protected the SNS members until they were forced to leave in the evening.[^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541]

On 24 March, thousands of protesters gathered in Belgrade to oppose plans to redevelop the [former Yugoslav army headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yugoslav_Ministry_of_Defence_Building), heavily damaged during the [1999 NATO bombing](322473.md), into a luxury complex financed by [Jared Kushner](6191053.md)'s firm, [Affinity Partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affinity_Partners). The Serbian government approved a multi-million-dollar contract with Kushner's company in 2024, granting a 99-year [lease](635681.md) on the land. Demonstrators demanded the restoration of the site's heritage status and the cancellation of the development project. Opposition to the redevelopment project extends beyond protesters. Serbian architects, engineers, and opposition parties voiced their disapproval, arguing that the site should remain a cultural monument rather than be developed for profit.[^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543]

On 26 March, students and professors blocked [Branko's Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branko's_Bridge) in Belgrade for three hours, protesting the pay cuts for the education workers.[^cite_note-544]

On 27 March, pro-government tabloid *Kurir* published an un-signed [op-ed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Op-ed) stating: "Stop harassing citizens. It might so happen that the citizens react differently to some future bridge blockades, than they have so far. That those who believed in \[pro-opposition sociologist] Jovo Bakić will swim in the river."[^cite_note-545][^cite_note-546][^cite_note-547]

On 28 March, students gathered in front of the pro-government TV station *Informer*, protesting the station's biased reporting and the [doxxing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doxxing) of protesters in its live studio program.[^cite_note-548][^cite_note-549]

On 29 March, students blocking the faculties of the University of Novi Sad announced the route of their planned cycling trip to [Strasbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strasbourg), with the idea of submitting a document on the violation of rights in Serbia to the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights).[^cite_note-550]

On 30 March, the Dean of the Faculty of Philosophy in [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1), Natalija Jovanović, who previously expressed her support for the students, was attacked by a woman with a knife, after which she was hospitalised.[^cite_note-551] In the Belgrade district of [Žarkovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDarkovo), a passenger car drove through the crowd during the 16 minute-long silent vigil and injured one person; the driver was arrested.[^cite_note-552][^cite_note-553] On the same day, protesters in [Užice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U%C5%BEice) blocked the local motorway; the blockade started with 16 minutes of silence.[^cite_note-554][^cite_note-555] In [Subotica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subotica), protesters gathered at the local Hippodrome, where they collected signatures in local communities so they could organize the first residents' assemblies ("Zbor"). From there, the protest participants set off along the Sombor Road; the column was led by tractors and bikers.[^cite_note-556][^cite_note-557]

On 31 March, students, professors and citizens gathered in Belgrade, Novi Sad and Niš to express their support for the assaulted dean.[^cite_note-558][^cite_note-559] President Vučić publicly called the dean "a criminal \[he] pardoned" and said the dean's injuries were "like a cut you get when cutting a cucumber or an onion".[^cite_note-560][^cite_note-561][^cite_note-562]

### Tour de Strasbourg

Main article: [Tour de Strasbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tour_de_Strasbourg)

On 3 April, 80 students embarked on a 1300km cycling journey from Belgrade to Strasbourg to deliver a letter to the European Union. The journey, titled "Tour de Strasbourg", was made to raise awareness within Europe about the protests in Serbia.[^cite_note-563] On their way, they were greeted by people on the streets including several major European cities such as Budapest (with the mayor of the city attending), Bratislava and Vienna, in which many Serbian diaspora live.[^cite_note-564] They arrived in Strasbourg on April 15 as planned.[^cite_note-565]

### Continuation of protests and preparations for the pro-government rally

On 9 April, in the evening hours, citizens from several Belgrade municipalities were protesting near [Vukov Spomenik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vukov_Spomenik) when a black [Volvo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volvo) drove through the crowd, injuring an undercover policeman; the driver was later arrested.[^cite_note-566][^cite_note-567]

The rally of the newly-formed "Movement for the People and the State", announced by president Vučić, was scheduled for 12 April in Belgrade. The news portal Nova.rs obtained an audio recording in which Nataša Marković, a member of the executive board of the state-owned [Poštanska štedionica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Po%C5%A1tanska_%C5%A1tedionica) bank, was allegedly heard pressuring employees to attend the rally, telling them that they should support "the state-owned bank which provides their daily bread."[^cite_note-568]

On 10 April, 20 plainclothes police officers entered the building of the Faculty of Medicine in [Niš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ni%C5%A1) and questioned around 30 students who had blocked the entrance to the higher education institution with benches. The students, who were detained at the Faculty in the afternoon hours, were summoned to give statements at the local Police Department. Citizens gathered in front of the Police Department to show support for the students.[^cite_note-569][^cite_note-570] Since the blockade continued, the police entered the Faculty building again on the next day. The protesters gathered in front of the Faculty to express their support for the blockade.[^cite_note-571]

During the month of April, a total of 16 [Croatian](5573.md) citizens with permanent residence in Serbia, have been declared a "threat to national security" due to their support of the protests on [social media](5897742.md) and were ordered to leave Serbia; in some cases, these Croatian citizens were [de facto](43594.md) forced to abandon their family members, who are Serbian citizens.[^cite_note-572][^cite_note-573] On 10 April, students organized a protest of support for the deported Croatian citizen, who was forced to leave her husband in Serbia.[^cite_note-574]

On 11 April, students from [Jagodina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagodina), [Ćuprija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86uprija), [Paraćin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Para%C4%87in) and Niš arrived in [Novi Pazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novi_Pazar) on foot, as did their fellow students who traveled by bicycle from Belgrade, Novi Sad and Niš. They were welcomed with red carpet by students from the State University in Novi Pazar, a day before the scheduled student protest in that city.[^cite_note-575][^cite_note-576]

On 12 April, Vučić held his rally "Ne damo Srbiju" ("We will not surrender Serbia") for the new movement with around 55.000 people attending.[^cite_note-577]

### The blockade of RTS and RTV



Building of [Radio Television of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Television_of_Serbia) blockaded by protesters, April 2025

In the evening hours of 14 April, the students called for an immediate action to block two public broadcasters RTS (both studios in Takovska and Košutnjak in Belgrade) and the studio of RTV in Novi Sad because they have not reported about the students cycling to Strasbourg. The main demand for the blockades was REM [Regulatorno telo za elektronske medije](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulatorno_telo_za_elektronske_medije?action=edit\&redlink=1) (The Regulatory Authority for Electronic Media) not having a board since November.[^cite_note-578] The next morning, some journalists were escorted by the police to work through the fast-food place nearby.[^cite_note-579]

On 15 April, as scheduled, the cyclists arrived at Strasbourg where they talked with the representatives of the [European commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_commission), giving them letters that detailed the situation in Serbia. They read one letter addressed to French president [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md).[^cite_note-580] Due to the blockades, RTS changed studios and reported from [Sremska TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sremska_TV?action=edit\&redlink=1)'s studio in Šid.[^cite_note-581]

On 16 April, the students announced that they will run from Novi Sad to Brussels from April 26 to May 11.[^cite_note-582] On the same day, a [new government](79706722.md) was formed with [Đuro Macut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90uro_Macut) as the prime minister.[^cite_note-583]

On 17 April, the rector of the [University of Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Belgrade), Vladan Đokić, was summoned to report to the police as a citizen. The summons was issued to collect information in the pre-investigation procedure for the criminal offense of abuse of office. The authorities have repeatedly called for the arrest of the rector Đokić and pointed to his alleged responsibility for the protests.[^cite_note-584][^cite_note-585] The rector was questioned on 18 April as part of a preliminary investigation; several thousand citizens gathered in support in front of the Criminal Police Directorate building in [New Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Belgrade).[^cite_note-586]

On 19 April, some employees of the RTS organized a Zbor, publicly asking the management of the public service and the relevant departments to urgently contact them and provide clear guidelines for work during the blockade. They stated that "If no one contacts us as soon as possible, we will consider that the management has withdrawn and is unable to manage the public media service. \[...] We demand an immediate change in the editorial concept of the RTS news program and professional reporting, in accordance with applicable laws and the journalistic code, which includes reporting on all current events in Serbia, which are of public interest to all citizens."[^cite_note-587]

On 22 April, a welcoming ceremony was organized for student cyclists, participants of the "Tour to Strasbourg", in Novi Sad. Educators, students and citizens gathered in front of the Ministry of Education in Belgrade, to protest the election of the new Minister of Education, Dejan Vuk Stanković. Student gatherings have also taken place in Niš and Kragujevac.[^cite_note-588][^cite_note-589]

On 23 April, for the first time, opposition councilors of the [Gornji Milanovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gornji_Milanovac) Municipal Assembly organized and hosted a local celebration of the Municipal Day – a memorial to the [Second Serbian Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Serbian_Uprising). The gathered citizens also had the opportunity to sign a petition with demands for the abolition of disciplinary proceedings against the local teachers, who took part in protest blockades; a review of the appointment of the new Minister of Education and a reduction in government pressure on the university and school community.[^cite_note-590] On the same day, the plenum of [Sremski Karlovci Gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karlovci_Gymnasium) graduates entered into a complete blockade, thereby expressing their solidarity with the students in the fight against corruption and promoting the rule of law in the country.[^cite_note-591]

On 25 April, President Vučić, appearing on *Informer TV*, said: "I'm afraid that something bad has started to appear within me, in my head, and that is - how to get back at all the people who have caused so much harm to me, my family, my children and everyone. And I can't do that, I can't do that to myself, I fight against it every day."[^cite_note-592][^cite_note-593][^cite_note-594]

### Marathon race to Brussels

On 25 April, 21 university students began a [relay race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relay_race) from Novi Sad, with the plan being to reach the [European Parliament](9581.md) session in 18 days. Their goal is to inform MEPs about the socio-political situation in Serbia.[^cite_note-595][^cite_note-596]

In [Novi Sad](113288.md), hundreds of high school pupils from [Belgrade](55904.md), [Jagodina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jagodina), [Subotica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subotica), [Užice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U%C5%BEice), [Valjevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valjevo) and [Vrbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrbas,_Serbia) walked on foot from their respective cities, to join the Novi Sad pupils for a large protests scheduled for the evening of 25 April.[^cite_note-597][^cite_note-598] The student support rallies continued in [Negotin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negotin), where the third zbor was also held.[^cite_note-599] In [Sremska Mitrovica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sremska_Mitrovica), citizens held a 16-minute silent vigil and afterwards blocked the local road for 5 hours.[^cite_note-600][^cite_note-601]

### Police attempts to disperse faculty blockade in Novi Sad

On 28 April, students blocking the Novi Sad faculties, together with gathered citizens, organized an action called "Morning Workout" in front of the Faculty of Sports. They blocked the dean, Patrik Drid, from entering the building. Soon, Drid appeared in front of the building with riot police. [Žandarmerija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%BDandarmerija) and the Police Intervention Unit in full riot gear began to push back students and citizens who were standing at the entrance to the faculty with shields and batons. Members of the [Ministry of Internal Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Internal_Affairs_\(Serbia\)) failed to break through the blockade in their first attempt. The gathered citizens called on the police to lower their shields and show that they are with the people. However, Žandarmerija members broke through to the entrance using [pepper spray](157302.md) and pushed back the citizens.[^cite_note-602][^cite_note-603] The police units failed to enter the building, and left the premises soon afterwards. On the same day, citizens gathered in the local Zbor organizations, with the support of the Faculty of Medicine, arrived at the Faculty of Sports, to support the students in the blockade.[^cite_note-604]

On the same day, the controversial bid for the selection of members of the REM Council (regulatory body overseeing the media) was canceled, and a new bid was announced; the student protesters announced that the blockade of RTS facilities was ending after 14 days.[^cite_note-605][^cite_note-606]

On 1 May, student protesters were joined by the Union of Independent Trade Unions of Serbia (SSSS), the United Branch Trade Union "Nezavisnost", the Association of Free and Independent Trade Unions (ASNS), the United Trade Unions of Serbia "Sloga" and the Confederation of Free Trade Unions. The protest was held in front of the [Serbian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_Government) building in Belgrade. The protesters presented demands for amendments to the Labor Law and the Law on Strike.[^cite_note-607][^cite_note-608]

On 5 May, employees of judicial institutions across Serbia went on strike. The judicial union announced earlier that their demands include decent wages, improved working conditions, clear recognition of the role that administrative and professional staff play in the functioning of the judiciary, and an end to the systematic underestimation of their work.[^cite_note-609][^cite_note-610]

### Demands for early elections

On 5 May, the student protesters called for early parliamentary elections, saying that "the authorities are not showing the slightest initiative" to meet their demands. The government that would be formed after the early elections should prepare fair and just conditions for holding next ones, and its mandate should be fixed-term. The students have no leaders and all issues, including appearances in the media, are decided by the plenums.[^cite_note-611] The student protesters announced a unified list of candidates to take part in the elections. Faculties will propose a certain number of their candidates to run for the student list, and each faculty will vet and talk with potential candidates. In Belgrade, where 51 faculties are currently in blockade, the idea is for each to propose two candidates and two reserves. The sorting algorithm will guarantee compliance with the Law on Gender Equality. The candidate names are kept secret until the elections are officially announced.[^cite_note-612] The opposition parties which supported the call for early elections are: Green-Left Front, Democratic Party (DS), Ecological Uprising, Kreni-promeni, PSG. The People's Movement of Serbia had been advocating for early elections even before the students' demands.[^cite_note-613]

On 9 May, [Novi Sad](113288.md) zbors organized a street blockade at 30 locations throughout the city, demanding local elections.[^cite_note-614] On 10 May, several zbors in [Kragujevac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kragujevac) organized a protest, urging the authorities to fix the derelict local overpass.[^cite_note-615] On 11 May, graduates of the [Zemun Gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zemun_Gymnasium) announced that they have physically blocked the school due to non-compliance with the agreement regarding the end of the school year, which, they claim, was adopted at the teachers' council. The decision to block was made at a plenum held on the same day.[^cite_note-616]

### Clashes in Kula

On 20 May, in [Kula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kula,_Serbia), a protest was held on the plateau in front of the Kula Municipality building, during which a banner with the inscription "Your [colour revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_revolution) has failed", which had been placed on the building earlier by SNS members, was removed from the Kula Municipality building and set on fire. At the same time, SNS supporters gathered in a nearby park, in front of the party premises. The gatherings were peaceful at first, but the situation escalated into a verbal conflict and afterwards into a physical confrontation. Police units in [riot gear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riot_gear) separated the opposing sides, however, at one point, SNS sympathizers used [fireworks](59493.md) aimed at the protesters.[^cite_note-617][^cite_note-618][^cite_note-619]

### Vidovdan protests

On 28 June, which is the date of [Vidovdan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vidovdan), one of the largest protest gatherings was held in Belgrade with an estimated 140,000 people in attendance according to independent monitors, although official figures reported around 36,000.[^cite_note-620][^cite_note-aljazeera-29-06-621][^cite_note-622] After speeches were held at Slavija Square in the afternoon, student organizers gave citizens approval to initiate social, political, and moral action, clarifying that it was not a call for violence but rather an encouragement not to remain passive in the face of injustice.[^cite_note-623]

Following the speeches, demonstrators marched towards the National Assembly, where clashes broke out with police. At least 77 protesters were detained, of whom 38 remained in custody facing criminal charges.[^cite_note-624][^cite_note-aljazeera-29-06-621] The confrontations were widely described as an instance of police brutality, reminiscent of the [2020 protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%932022_Serbian_protests).[^cite_note-625]

On 29 June, in response to the previous day's events and what protesters described as inadequate police conduct, demonstrations escalated into acts of [civil disobedience](37073.md). Protesters set up barricades using improvised objects such as fences, containers, and tires, repeatedly crossed pedestrian walkways, held impromptu protests on major roads, and in some cases pelted offices of the ruling Serbian Progressive Party with eggs. The barricades initially emerged in Zemun, where the student group based in Belgrade declared: "Zemun reacts. It's time for all municipalities to organize. Civil disobedience."[^cite_note-626] Similar actions soon spread to other parts of Belgrade and across Serbia, including road and bridge blockades organized as part of a decentralized campaign of student-led disobedience.[^cite_note-627][^cite_note-628][^cite_note-629] Protests also spread outside the capital, with solidarity blockades and demonstrations reported in Novi Sad, Kragujevac, and other towns, where local communities—including farmers, veterans, and civic groups—joined students in erecting barricades and roadblocks.[^cite_note-630]

On 30 June, Serbian police moved to clear barricades erected by protesters throughout Belgrade. Metal fences and garbage containers placed in areas such as Zemun were dismantled, with several dozen demonstrators being detained; video footage showed riot officers carrying a young man into a police van by his arms and legs.[^cite_note-631] Protesters repeatedly rebuilt roadblocks and attempted to sustain their actions by walking across pedestrian crossings to disrupt traffic even after police removal efforts.[^cite_note-632] The wave of civil resistance continued into 1 July, when a court released eight students who had been charged with plotting to "overthrow the state", pending trial. Roadblocks and demonstrations persisted despite the legal developments.[^cite_note-633]

On 2–3 July, reports indicated that police in riot gear attempted to enter the Faculty of Law at the University of Belgrade—detaining approximately 23 students. Former rector Branko Kovačević condemned the action as a clear violation of the institution's legally guaranteed autonomy. The police, however, denied entering faculty premises, asserting that they respected university autonomy.[^cite_note-634][^cite_note-635]

Across several cities—Belgrade, Novi Sad, Niš, and Novi Pazar—the police continued to suppress protests. On 3 July, at least 79 demonstrators, including university students, were detained during the break-up of roadblocks. Human rights groups and the Council of Europe expressed concern over alleged excessive use of force, arbitrary arrests, and numerous injuries sustained by demonstrators.[^cite_note-636][^cite_note-637][^cite_note-638]

In early July, the city of [Užice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U%C5%BEice) became one of the focal points of the protest movement. On 6 July, around 350 citizens broke through a police cordon and sat on the main road linking [Požega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Po%C5%BEega,_Croatia) with [Zlatibor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zlatibor_\(town\)). Two people were arrested that evening, prompting citizens to gather in front of the local police station.[^cite_note-639] The following day, 7 July, a large protest drew participants from Užice and nearby towns such as [Požega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Po%C5%BEega,_Croatia), [Kosjerić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosjeri%C4%87) and [Čačak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%8Ca%C4%8Dak), who again broke through police and gendarmerie lines to block the highway toward Zlatibor. After the occupation of the road, police forces eventually withdrew, though seven more people were later arrested on charges of violent conduct.[^cite_note-640][^cite_note-641] On 13 July, thousands from across Serbia gathered in Užice under the slogan "Serbia, Užice is calling you". The protest, which was peaceful, included a speech by University of Belgrade rector Đokić, and blocked the highway toward Zlatibor. This time, police did not intervene.[^cite_note-642] The following day, the Užice court overturned the detention orders, releasing all of those arrested.[^cite_note-643]

Reports from the Vidovdan-related protests detailed testimonies of protesters who sustained serious injuries during police interventions, such as broken bones and head trauma allegedly caused by gendarmes and plainclothes officers. Between 28 June and 14 July, hundreds of arrests and numerous injuries were recorded across several Serbian cities. Legal experts criticized the police for disproportionate use of force, failure to properly identify themselves, and violations of arrest procedures. Observers also highlighted a broader pattern of impunity for police violence, noting that Serbia had previously been found liable in multiple [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) rulings that resulted in compensation to victims but no criminal accountability for individual officers.[^cite_note-644]

### High-profile arrests

On 1 August 2025, by order of the Public Prosecutor's Office for Organised Crime, a major arrest operation was launched in connection with the Novi Sad canopy collapse. Former Minister [Tomislav Momirović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomislav_Momirovi%C4%87) was among 11 individuals detained on suspicion of corruption related to the reconstruction works of the station and the wider Budapest–Belgrade railway modernization project.[^cite_note-645] In a televised broadcast, Vučić publicly raised concerns about what he described as "selective justice" and accused the head of the Organised Crime Prosecutor's Office of acting at the behest of unspecified "Europeans."[^cite_note-646]

### August street violence

Beginning on 12 August, anti-government protests in [Vrbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrbas,_Serbia) and [Bačka Palanka](283875.md) turned increasingly confrontational. Demonstrators were attacked by masked groups wielding pyrotechnics, bottles, and rocks—the police only minimally responded to these attacks. According to the [Civil Rights Defenders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Defenders), "police actively protected masked goons who brutally attacked citizens with iron rods, flares, rocks, and truncheons," while at times officers themselves engaged in excessive force instead of maintaining public order.[^cite_note-647]

These events sparked a wave of unrest across Serbia. From 14 to 16 August, amid rising repression, protests escalated with demonstrators demolishing offices of the SNS, SPS, and SRS, first in Novi Sad, then across the country—often clashing directly with police protecting these sites. Protesters spray-painted, vandalized, and defaced party buildings, while riot police deployed tear-gas and forcefully dispersed crowds. Injuries were reported among at least five officers and numerous civilians, and arrests occurred nationwide.[^cite_note-648]

In a controversial move, members of the military police unit Cobras were deployed to protect the SNS office of party leader Miloš Vučević in Novi Sad. Legal experts warned that such a deployment breached military neutrality laws by politicizing the armed forces. Seven Cobras were reportedly injured in the ensuing clash, and one officer fired a warning shot into the air.[^cite_note-649]

[Valjevo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valjevo) became the scene of one of the most violent episodes of the protests when police forces carried out a large-scale crackdown against civilians. Video footage showed groups of officers kicking and beating a man lying on the ground, who was later assaulted again while in custody. Dozens of people were dragged out of taxis, bakeries, betting shops, and other premises and beaten, while rubber bullets were reportedly fired near groups of minors. Witnesses stated that boys as young as 15 were assaulted, including one who was kicked and struck by at least ten officers while lying on the pavement. The city hospital treated around sixty injured individuals, four of them with serious injuries, while police reported 19 of their officers injured and confirmed 17 arrests, several of them underage.[^cite_note-650][^cite_note-651][^cite_note-652] Interior Minister Ivica Dačić later claimed that he had "spent hours trying to identify" the minor allegedly beaten in Valjevo, ultimately concluding that no individual matching the widely circulated footage could be located in hospital or police records—a move he used to challenge the validity of earlier reports.[^cite_note-653] Following this, protesters razed the SNS office to the ground and set fire to municipal and judicial buildings. The demonstration, sparked by allegations of police brutality, drew several thousand participants.[^cite_note-654][^cite_note-655]

During the crackdown across Serbia, a female student alleged she was subjected to a violent assault by the commander of the protective unit, Marko Kričko, who reportedly struck her, slammed her head against a wall, threatened rape, and destroyed her personal belongings while holding her on the floor for hours.[^cite_note-656]

Western outlets increasingly focused on allegations of police brutality. *The Guardian* documented the aggressive use of force and tear gas during crackdowns in Belgrade, while Radio Free Europe reported that the initially peaceful protests had devolved into daily clashes fueled by perceptions of systemic state violence.[^cite_note-657][^cite_note-658] An editorial by *The Guardian* called on the EU to urgently prioritize Serbia's democratic crisis in light of these "widespread reports of police brutality," warning that silence could destabilize the region.[^cite_note-659]

### September

On 1 September, a silent march was held by high-school students in Belgrade in memory of the victims of the canopy collapse. Similar marches were held in Kragujevac and Novi Sad.[^cite_note-660] On 5 September, police fired tear gas and charged at protesters demanding free elections and Vucic's resignation during a rally in Novi Sad.[^cite_note-661]

On 6 September, the 2025 edition of the [Belgrade Pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgrade_Pride) march was held, which included banners featuring slogans of the student movement and the phrase "Gays against police state!".[^cite_note-662]

### October

| --- | --- |

A nationwide march was held on 1 October to commemorate 11 months since the Novi Sad canopy collapse.[^cite_note-663]

On 13 October, the physical blockade of the [Fifth Belgrade Gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Belgrade_Gymnasium?action=edit\&redlink=1) by school students began. The reason for this is the unprofessional and incompetent management of the gymnasium by acting director Danka Nešović for several months. The students demanded her immediate dismissal, as well as the return of professors whose employment contracts were not extended.

On 14 October, the acting director of the Fifth Belgrade Gymnasium, Danka Nešović, declared that the school is under a "state of siege". Minister of Education [Dejan Vuk Stanković](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dejan_Vuk_Stankovi%C4%87) spoke out, saying that the blockade of the school was an act of violence and pressure.

On 22 October, the [European Parliament](9581.md) adopted a resolution condemning the growing wave of repression in Serbia, calling for sanctions against high-ranking officials accused of violence and human rights violations. They called to account for undermining democratic institutions, spreading pro-Russian propaganda and using the judiciary to suppress dissenting opinions. Concern was expressed about the Serbian government's attempt to weaken independent media that report objectively. The authorities have been called to fully clarify the case of the canopy collapse, and to sanction those responsible. Camp "[Ćaciland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86aciland_Protest_Camp)" was highlighted as illegal.

On the same day, a person attacked [Ćaciland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86aciland_Protest_Camp), setting fire to some of the tents in front of the [House of the National Assembly](39875666.md). The authorities and institutions pointed out that the attacker was part of the student uprisings, and that the case was an act of [terrorism](30636.md). However, it is believed that the attacker is close to the government, and that the event was planned in advance to divert attention away from the adoption of the European Parliament resolution.

### November

The commemorative gathering in Novi Sad on the anniversary of the train station canopy collapse was held on 1 November. An estimated 110,000 people were present.[^cite_note-AJS-SNS-5-NOV-2025-60]

On 2 November, Dijana Hrka, the mother of Stefan Hrka, one of those killed in the collapse of the canopy, started a hunger strike in front of the [National Assembly](39875666.md), i.e. the tent-settlement of [Ćaciland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86aciland_Protest_Camp), from which a group of people who support the government and live there started their daily provocations. Despite peaceful gatherings of citizens in support of Dijana, people from Ćaciland pelted protesters with pyrotechnics, chairs and lasers and played loud "patriotic" music until late at night, after midnight.

On 10 November, bus driver Milomir Jaćimović, who regularly transported people to protests for free, also went on hunger strike with his son, because the police confiscated two of his vehicles while he was transporting people to a protest in support of Dijana Hrka in [Belgrade](55904.md).

On 17 November, Dijana Hrka ended her hunger strike in front of the [Assembly](39875666.md).

On 20 November, a physical attack on [N1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N1_\(TV_channel\)) television reporters occurred in front of the [Ćaciland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86aciland_Protest_Camp) tent-settlement by an unknown person who destroyed the camera and physically attacked the cameraman. [The police](11486556.md) did not react to the attack.

On 22 November, a protest was held, marking one year since the attack on the students of the [Faculty of Dramatic Arts (FDU)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faculty_of_Dramatic_Arts,_University_of_Arts_in_Belgrade) in [Belgrade](55904.md). The protest column moved from FDU, through [New Belgrade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Belgrade), [Branko's Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branko's_Bridge), [the Palace of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palata_pravde?action=edit\&redlink=1), all the way to the building of [Radio Television of Serbia (RTS)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Television_of_Serbia).

On 24 November, a protest was held in front of the [Assembly](39875666.md) in support of N1 television and its reporters who were attacked a few days before.

On 30 November, [local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Serbian_local_elections) were held in [Mionica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mionica), [Sečanj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Se%C4%8Danj) and [Negotin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negotin). Numerous irregularities were noted, and the [Serbian Progressive Party (SNS)](19454384.md) won in all three municipalities with a significant drop (up to 30%) compared to the [previous elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Serbian_local_elections).

### 2026

In May 2026, the student movement published a memorandum outlining its position on the status of Kosovo.[^cite_note-664] The memorandum stated that Kosovo and Metohija are an integral and inalienable part of Serbia, describing this position as based on the Constitution of Serbia, historical continuity and international law. It also argued that the issue of Kosovo should be addressed through cooperation with relevant international organisations and in accordance with Serbia's Constitution and international law.[^cite_note-665]

The publication of the memorandum prompted widespread public debate and differing reactions from government officials, opposition figures, analysts and civil society.[^cite_note-666]

On 27 June 2026, Aleksandar Vučić announced his intent to resign, saying he will 'resign within weeks'.[^cite_note-667]

## Symbols and slogans



А red handprint, a symbol commonly used by the protesters.

[^cite_note-668]



Red handprints with *You have blood on your hands!* ([Serbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaj's_Latin_alphabet): *Krvave su vam ruke!*) text on the left

A common protester slogan has been "corruption kills".[^cite_note-Delauney-2024-87] Protest symbols included red handprints with the caption "*You have blood on your hands!*", referring to the authorities and ruling politicians, and bleeding doves, with the dove being one of the symbols of Novi Sad.[^cite_note-AP_News-2024a-73][^cite_note-669] Various banners could be seen during the protests and blockades. Most of them mention Vučić and other members of the ruling party, the public prosecutor [Zagorka Dolovac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zagorka_Dolovac?action=edit\&redlink=1), and generally express support for the students. Some of the banners feature quotes or references from Serbian and pop culture, such as *[Better Call Saul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Better_Call_Saul)* ("Not Even [Saul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saul_Goodman) Will Save You"), *[Grand Theft Auto VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Theft_Auto_VI)* ("The People Rose Up Before \[the Release of] *GTA VI*"), *[Game of Thrones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_of_Thrones)*' [Daenerys Targaryen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daenerys_Targaryen), [Charli XCX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charli_XCX)'s album *[Brat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brat_\(album\))*, among others.[^cite_note-670] National [Serbian flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_flags), both contemporary and historical, as well as the flags of many Serbian cities and municipalities, and university and faculty flags, are common sights at protests.[^cite_note-671] [Ferrari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrari) flags and paraphernalia, once a prominent symbol of opposition to the Milošević regime, have also made a return.[^cite_note-672] Students also often call out Vučić's excessive behavior, arguing that he is acting *[ultra vires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra_vires)* by involving himself in matters outside his legal capacity as President of Serbia.[^cite_note-673]

Some banners also include the word "*ćaci*" ("ћаци"), a reference to the [anti-protest graffiti *ćaci u školu*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%86aci_u_%C5%A1kolu); this was largely seen as an attempt by the ruling SNS to provoke the students, but it backfired because of the creator's typo. The pro-government encampment in Pioneers Park was widely dubbed *ćacilend* by protesters. Another catchphrase which caught on, originating from the social news aggregate [Reddit](3829005.md), is "pump it" ("pumpaj"), meaning to "keep increasing the pressure", which also found its place in memes and banners.[^cite_note-674] Another closely related phrase, "stew it" ("dinstaj"), was popularized by sociologist [Jovo Bakić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jovo_Baki%C4%87).[^cite_note-675]

## Reactions

Leading up to the 15 March and 28 June protests in Belgrade, public transit operations in Belgrade were halted. Most private bus companies cancelled most departures to and from Belgrade one day prior to both protests. At the same time, passenger trains coming in and out of Belgrade were halted due to alleged bomb threats.[^cite_note-676][^cite_note-677][^cite_note-678][^cite_note-679] An attempt of Niš students to organise transport to Belgrade failed after a private bus company allegedly received threats from people close to the government not to transport students.[^cite_note-680]

### Criticism of protests

Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) voiced support for the Serbian authorities and rejected what he called a "[colour revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_revolution)".[^cite_note-681]

The EU Commission enlargement director-general, Gert Jan Koopman, stated that the EU "will not accept or support a violent change of power in Serbia."[^cite_note-682]

US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s envoy for special missions, [Richard Grenell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Grenell), wrote on X that the United States supports peaceful demonstrations but does not support "those who undermine the rule of law or who forcefully take over government buildings."[^cite_note-N1-2025e-683][^cite_note-684]

### Neutral stance

The [European Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_der_Leyen_Commission_II) and the [Council of the European Union](5866.md) have neither supported nor condemned the protests.[^cite_note-685][^cite_note-686] On 28 January 2025, a group of Serbian public figures sent a letter to EU officials, including European Commission President [Ursula von der Leyen](2991932.md), urging them to become more actively involved in supporting a "free, democratic and European Serbia".[^cite_note-European_Western_Balkans-2025-48][^cite_note-687]

Many Serbs in support of the protests have interpreted this lack of attention as a silent backing of the Vučić government. For example, several days after "15. za 15" protest in Belgrade, Vučić met with European Commissioner for Enlargement [Marta Kos](77809600.md) in Brussels, a meeting the EU diplomat described as "constructive." Kos made no mention of the use of a sound cannon against non-violent protesters the weekend before, drawing criticism from protesters and their supporters.[^cite_note-688] Support for EU membership has fallen in Serbia since the protests began, with many Serbs in support of the protests viewing the EU as an opponent of democratization and bureaucratic reform efforts,[^cite_note-689] resulting in EU flags and other pro-European symbols being noticeably absent from rallies.[^cite_note-690]

### Domestic support

Protests were publicly supported by over 5,000 university professors, cooperators and researchers.[^cite_note-691]

### Support from neighboring countries and the Serbian diaspora

Since mid-December protests have been held by students in [Bosnia and Herzegovina](3463.md), [Montenegro](20760.md), [Croatia](5573.md), [Slovenia](27338.md), [North Macedonia](23564616.md) and by Serbian diaspora in numerous cities around the world. On 2 February 2025, during a protest gathering in front of the [Embassy of Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_Serbia,_Budapest) in Budapest, an unknown man inside the embassy [gave the middle finger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_finger) to the protesters from the open window. Ambassador [Aleksandra Đurović](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandra_%C4%90urovi%C4%87) declined to either apologize or reveal the identity of the man.[^cite_note-950]

### International support

Vasilije Čarapić, a Montenegrin politician and member of the leading [Europe Now!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe_Now!) party, publicly expressed support for the students. In his statement, Čarapić emphasized the importance of regional solidarity and the need for accountability in infrastructure projects, urging governments to prioritize public safety to prevent such tragedies.[^cite_note-951]

Former president of [Georgia](48768.md) [Salome Zourabichvili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salome_Zourabichvili) who is currently leading the [2024–2025 Georgian protests](78322562.md) supported the Serbian protests by calling them "similar"[^cite_note-952] and also called for the stronger support from the EU for protests in Serbia.[^cite_note-953]

European parties [Progressive Alliance of Socialists and Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_Alliance_of_Socialists_and_Democrats) (S\&D), [European Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Green_Party), [Renew Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renew_Europe) and [European Left](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Left) supported the protests.[^cite_note-954][^cite_note-955][^cite_note-956] On 31 January, it was announced that the students of Serbia were a candidate for [2025 Nobel Peace Prize](78880914.md).[^cite_note-957]

Following March 15 protest in Belgrade, a group of 210 academics from Europe, the United States and South America signed a letter of support to Serbian students and academics. The list included [Étienne Balibar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Balibar), [Judith Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judith_Butler), [Annie Ernaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Ernaux), [Nancy Fraser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Fraser), [Thomas Piketty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Piketty) and [Slavoj Žižek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavoj_%C5%BDi%C5%BEek).[^cite_note-958]

#### Public figures, artists and celebrities

Other public figures who publicly supported the protests include:
